unconsciously translates what he hears into something that he can
understand. I would rather be reported by my bitterest enemy among
philosophers than by a friend innocent of philosophy." Which is not to
say that Domscheit's portrait of Assange as a megalomaniac asshole is
wrong, because from all the other coverage of him, it's clear there's
a lot of truth to this portrayal.

Domscheit's personal failings are only highlighted by the
since-complete and unmitigated failure of his 'OpenLeaks' project.

It's also bizarrely lacking in technical details, which is the one part
one would hope a supposed geek like Domscheit would at least make sure
his book got right! (Probably also thanks to the journalist.)

Still, many interesting bits. I remember thinking way back that for a
group claiming so many participants and advisers, it had an oddly low flow
of leaks---which Domscheit says was because it was mostly him and
Assange; makes sense. I was amused to learn that the Iceland laws were
based by Assange in part on Stephenson's *Cryptonomicon*---again, makes
sense in a curious way.
:::

*The Bridge to Lucy Dunne*, Exurb1a 2016
----------------------------------------

[[*[The Bridge to Lucy Dunne](https://www.amazon.com/Bridge-Lucy-Dunne-Exurb1a-ebook/dp/B01F7IQEHC)*]{.book-review-title}[Exurb1a]{.book-review-author}[2016]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
18 short stories typically with an ironic SF or fantasy tinge and heavy reliance on twist endings (more like _Sum: Forty Tales from the Afterlives_ or Chiang's [_Stories of Your Life and Others_](#stories-of-your-life-and-others-chiang-2010) than [_The Corpse Exhibition: And Other Stories of Iraq_](#the-corpse-exhibition-and-other-stories-of-iraq-blasim-2014)). As with most anthologies, the quality is uneven.

Decent:

- "The Guests": self-consistent time travel setting; no one will be
  surprised by the ending
- "Tap": family drama/horror
- "Into That Good Night"
- "VASE": SF thinkpiece; reminds me a great deal of Ted Chiang's
  "Liking What You See"
- "My Brother, My Wife, and the Wheel": Icelandic supernatural horror;
  probably too long, though
- "Sins: Various"
- "The First Living Exhibit": overwritten but still amusing
- "Silicon Nights": the best story in the collection, an homage to
  *The Arabian Nights* in a fast-forward Vingean-like setting (Arabian
  in the story structure, not the setting itself, so not like
  Rajaniemi's *The Causal Angel* if that's what you were thinking)
  where the twist really does work

Poor:

- "A Dance": cosmological allegory; done before and better (eg. ["The
  Goddess of Everything
  Else"](https://slatestarcodex.com/2015/08/17/the-goddess-of-everything-else-2/),
  and of course *Star Maker*)
- "Godspeed and Goodnight": time-dilation horror, overly long, not
  nearly as good as Watts's time-dilation horror
- "The Flowers". "Collision": absolutely predictable
- "Empty Cups", "The Bridge to Lucy Dunne": failed attempts at regular
  literature
- "The Bait": moderately interesting but I was unsure if I understood
  the twist ending or what it was supposed to be
- "A Haunting"
- "The Rite"
- "The Gift"
:::

*The Japanese Family Storehouse*, Ihara 1959
--------------------------------------------

[[*[The Japanese Family Storehouse: Or the Millionaire's Gospel Modernised](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0521061822&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Saikaku Ihara]{.book-review-author}[1959]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
One of the few Ihara Saikaku works available in English (albeit long out
of print), the stories turn out to be extremely short and only tenuously
linked (even allowing for haikai-style linking as Sargent repeatedly
tries to defend the stories), and I enjoyed them little as works as
fiction rather than anecdotes of Edo economic life.

Most of the book is Sargent's commentary and ancillary material, and it
is well done: detailed commentary on the many
deeply-Edo-period/chōnin-specific obscurities and allusions, a translation
of the original *Millionaire's Gospel* espousing townsmen virtues, and
a large foldout map. I spent more time flipping to the back and reading
the commentaries than the stories.

What I think is good about this book is that after plowing one's way
through the commentary, one has a thick sense of the vibrant
complexities and customs and superstitions and ferment of
commerce/technology in the prosperous and peaceful Edo period, with a
particularly Osakan perspective, from the appropriate fish to display at
New Years to the best way for scoundrels to exploit bankruptcy to the
casual warnings against fornicating too much with young boy-actors to
allusions to the famous [Dōjima rice futures
market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D%C5%8Djima_Rice_Exchange) (for
more on Dōjima, see ["Private Ordering at the World's First Futures
Exchange", West
2000](/doc/economics/2000-west.pdf)---it was
strikingly like the Dutch VOC stock/futures market described by [Joseph
de la Vega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_de_la_Vega) in his 1688
[*Confusion of
Confusions*](/doc/economics/1688-delavega-confusionofconfusions.pdf)).
:::

*The Pillow Book*, Shōnagon 2006 {#the-pillow-book-shonagon-2006}
--------------------------------

[[*[The Pillow Book](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0140448063&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Sei Shōnagon]{.book-review-author}[2006]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
While the descriptions of natural beauty are admirable, and some of the
anecdotes of court life are interesting, much of the material is boring
and Shonagon herself has ugly streaks of elitism in her outright
contempt for anyone lower than herself (eg. casually declaring that
lower-class women should not even be allowed long or medium-length hair,
an opinion which is certainly not 'delightful') and fawning admiration
over anyone higher than her, particular the thoroughly unimpressive
emperor/empress, and her endless fascination with the emblems of rank
such as expensive clothing, which she apparently considers to be the
full measure of a human and little else about them requires
description. One could write a devastating style parody of Shonagon as a teenager: "[72] And then, like, it was so delightful when Britney wore a cute tartan skirt paired with a hot pink handbag, a purple barrette, white socks, brown loafers, a Chinese-style jacket over it, and all the other cheerleaders could hardly stop themselves squealing over how gorgeous she was and I just had to write a poem on Snapchat. [73] List of the cutest boys in our school: ..."

For a skilled poet and one with such recall of Chinese and Japanese
classics, it's distressing to see how little insight she apparently has
into anything and anyone, how much learning without wisdom; the court
and its pretensions and hanger-ons surely invited sharp criticism, or at
least some awareness of its faults, but Shonagon manages to conceal any
such insight. No wonder Murasaki Shikibu thought little of her. The
translator warns that

> Similar opinions have continued to be expressed down the centuries,
> and modern scholars (men) have often been equally irritated by her.
> She has been dismissed by some as a mere chatterbox of a woman, and
> _The Pillow Book_ considered to be nothing more than a silly
> gentlewoman's idle thoughts spilling themselves haphazardly on to the
> page. It is common in Japan to contrast her with Murasaki Shikibu, and
> those who side with Sei Shōnagon in this perceived rivalry are often
> characterized as vacuous and frivolous.

Indeed.
:::

*Robert Bakewell and the Longhorn Breed of Cattle*, Stanley 1998
----------------------------------------------------------------

[[*[Robert Bakewell and the Longhorn Breed of Cattle](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0852363052&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Pat Stanley]{.book-review-author}[1998]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
([ebook](/doc/genetics/selection/artificial/1995-stanley-robertbakewellandthelonghornbreedofcattle.pdf "'Robert Bakewell and the Longhorn Breed of Cattle', Stanley 1995"))
*Robert Bakewell and the Longhorn Breed of Cattle*, Stanley 1995, is one
of the few books on [Robert
Bakewell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Bakewell_(agriculturalist)),
who I discuss in my essay/book review ["Origins of Innovation: Bakewell
& Breeding"](/review/bakewell "'Origins of Innovation: Bakewell & Breeding', Gwern 2018").

While Bakewell is better known for his sheep, Pat Stanley was a local
cattle enthusiast affiliated with the New Dishley Society dedicated to
Bakewell in the UK, and so the first half of the book provides a short
biography of Bakewell, assisted by local details like his neglected
grave marker, while the second half follows the longhorn cattle up to
the 20^th^ century.

Stanley is unabashed about her admiration of Bakewell, so this is not a
critical biography like Russell 1986's *Like Engend'ring Like: Heredity
and Animal Breeding in Early Modern England*, which is skeptical about
how much better Bakewell's breeds were, but it includes details about
Bakewell that I don't think I saw in the primary/second sources I have
collated in the Wikipedia article, like how his sister managed the
estate--I *had* wondered since he didn't seem to be married---and when
she died.

The second half is of much less interest to me, as it quickly gets into
details of longhorns I have no idea about, and lacks clear organization,
devolving into long lists and accounts of cattle auctions, leavened by
illustrations of famous cattle. I didn't learn anything from the second
half.
:::

*Hive Mind*, Jones 2015
-----------------------

[[*[Hive Mind: How Your Nation's IQ Matters So Much More Than Your Own](https://www.amazon.com/Hive-Mind-Your-Nations-Matters/dp/0804785961/)*]{.book-review-title}[Garett Jones]{.book-review-author}[2015]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Pop sci, which reads more like overgrown blog posts. Very weak overview
of IQ's connection to income: poor overview of what IQ is, all its
correlates, the evidence establishing its causal role like the
iodization historical studies (which I think are extremely important yet
there's not even allusions to their results), and surprisingly brief
coverage of the cross-national correlational and longitudinal
regressions (which you would think would be discussed at length). Jones
pretty much doesn't discuss core issues like measurement error of IQ
and income, and he is shockingly naively optimistic about the prospects
of boosting global IQ---he takes the Flynn effect fully at face value,
ignores education signaling (this, from a colleague of Caplan...?), and
totally ignores the technical issues about IQ gains typically resulting
from loss of validity of the test, publication bias, short-term gains
fading out, and the almost total failure to find meaningful intelligence
boosts from anything other than parasite eradication and iron and iodine
supplementation---which have been largely done for most countries...

I am not surprised that I learned little from the book, but I am
disappointed that it is so superficial and scattershot and I cannot link
it to other people as a good explanation of why IQs matter so much to
people and countries and why we should put very high valuations on
charitable projects like iodization.

See also https://slatestarcodex.com/2015/12/08/book-review-hive-mind/ /
https://slatestarcodex.com/2015/12/10/list-of-passages-i-highlighted-in-my-copy-of-hive-mind/
:::

*The City of Falling Angels*, Berendt 2006
------------------------------------------

[[*[The City of Falling Angels](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0143036939&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[John Berendt]{.book-review-author}[2006]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
An American writer with access to Venetian high society moves there and
writes down all the gossip and good stories he witnesses or hears about
while living there for a few years. This book has all the strengths and
weaknesses that this sounds like: on the one hand, these are pretty much
all stories I am completely unfamiliar with since the reflections of
Venice in things I have read or seen are typically of pre-20 century
Venice (think stories like *The Count of Monte Cristo* or film works
like *Aria* or histories of the Mediterranean region) and so stories
like theaters burning or the Save Venice feuds or the Pound scandal are
news; on the other hand, they were news to me because I didn't care
about any of them before and I don't care much about them having read
*Falling Angels* either. As one might expect, there's not much
cohesiveness to the stories beyond the narrator himself because real
life is not so cooperative as to combine all the storylines into a
single satisfying conclusion.

On the positive side: Berendt is a fine writer who smoothly narrates
events and lets people speak for themselves more than most writers
would. He is aware of the danger of relying on gossip and seems to keep
an open mind as he critically examines all the versions and stories
he's told, and he seems to have spoken to everyone so he has plenty to
compare. He also has a good eye for details and interesting people
and anecdotes. I don't regret reading it, even if I don't expect to
ever re-read it nor learned any 'big' truths or stories.
:::

*Structural Equation Modeling*, Lee 2007
----------------------------------------

[[*[Structural Equation Modeling](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0470024232&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Sik-Yum Lee]{.book-review-author}[2007]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Heavily mathematical treatment of SEMs, with little attempt at catering
to beginners or non-mathematicians; all implementation details are
purged from the text and segregated to a website (which was less than
helpful when I got around to reading it during a period of no Internet).
It's hard to see who the formalism would be useful for: who is so
intimately familiar with the math and notation and linear algebra that they
could follow this text's exposition but don't know anything about
SEMs? I didn't get much out of it except a sense that ML estimation
really struggles with any remotely complex model that I could fit
without a second thought in JAGS.

And despite usually taking a high-level mathematical approach, it
devotes a peculiar amount of space to depicting convergence plots and
noting how many samples it took the MCMC to converge, which is something
that is usually of the least importance especially since none of the
models seem to take more than a few thousand iterations to converge,
which was a trivial amount of computation in 2007 and is even more
trivial now. Some other aspects of the modeling struck me as
questionable: repeatedly the models are analyzed by a peculiar procedure
in which noninformative priors are used to analyze 10% and then the
posterior is reused as the prior for the other 90% of the data---which
should be completely pointless a procedure since it should deliver
exactly the same final result, but the author apparently thinks it's
worth doing.
:::

*The Autobiography Of Benvenuto Cellini*, Cellini 1999
------------------------------------------------------

[[*[The Autobiography Of Benvenuto Cellini](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0140447180&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Benvenuto Cellini]{.book-review-author}[1999]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
To read this, one wonders how Cellini survived to age 20, much less age
70! He is constantly killing and being attacked, wenching his models,
contracting hideous illnesses (or noting in passing the constant
unexpected death of others), and being betrayed (by this account) or
insulting others. It's an endless exhausting cycle such that even
Cellini had to notice its futility and danger. One has to wonder how
much he exaggerates: aside from the demonology and weather-controlling,
it seems so routine for people to go around armed and attacking for
minor insults and then dying of a scratch. Then there is his strange
attitude to his patrons: on the one hand, he seems largely unable to
criticize them or the system despite wallowing in their corruption and
wealth (surely the King of France wasn't all *that*, and given the
sheer servility and ignobility and criminality of the popes he deals with,
his tolerance of them is astounding), but on the other hand, he almost
goes out of his way to mess with them.

Well, it's fun in small doses, the constant tumult of Cellini's life
suggests that the constant murder and assault and theft and large gifts we
read of in picaresques or stories (like the _Decameron_) are much more
realistic than we give them credit for, and it's pretty cool to look at
the Wikipedia article and see images of the works he labors over at such
length in his autobiography.
:::

*Newton and the Counterfeiter*, Levenson 2009
---------------------------------------------

[[*[Newton and the Counterfeiter: The Unknown Detective Career of the World's Greatest Scientist](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0151012784&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Thomas Levenson]{.book-review-author}[2009]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
A quick breezy read good for an evening; Levenson touches on the
highlights of Newton's early life and throughout keeps an eye out for the
telling detail or quote which might bring the past to life for us, is
sympathetic towards the alchemy and tries to put it in a context, and
then plunges into Newton's war with an obscure counterfeiter.

This section would make good background reading for Neal Stephenson's
*Baroque Cycle* (despite Levenson's book being published in 2009 and
the cycle finished in 2004, and the latter being one of the few places a
reader might have encountered Newton in the same breath as
counterfeiters, entirely unmentioned), as it pretty clearly explains the
monetary issues of clipping, recoining, balance of exchange with Asia,
etc, in a less digressive and action-packed manner than Stephenson's
doorstoppers.

It's interesting that Newton was so involved in criminal matters, but
in some respects Levenson is trying to wring blood from a stone: Newton
doesn't seem to have been any Sherlock Holmes (an apt comparison since
Moriarty was an expert on Newtonian mechanics), but rather, just
obsessive and hardworking and applying all the standard investigative
techniques. The story is hampered also by the relatively low amount of
documentation (one wonders what the boxes of documents Newton burned
would have revealed).
:::

*Drug Interdiction*, Steffan 2010
---------------------------------

[[*[Drug Interdiction: Partnerships, Legal Principles, and Investigative Methodologies for Law Enforcement, Second Edition](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=142009176X&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[George S. Steffan]{.book-review-author}[2010]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
An inside view of how to run the War on Drugs for the boots on the
ground. If you can manage to stand the unctuous and self-congratulatory
tone and its brazenness in pretending that civil asset seizure is
anything but a travesty, you will find it's an informative overview of
how cops approach drug busts and what limits are on them, and
confirmation of much of the common wisdom (for example, that the private
carriers like UPS or FedEx have zero interest in standing up for their
customers); I don't think I'm spoiling anything when I reveal that the
book's advice for each transportation modality can be boiled down to
(1) cultivate informants among regular staff; and (2) trick or
intimidate as many people as possible into agreeing to searches of
themselves or their homes or possessions.

Those interested in the DNMs should read [Chapter 3: "Knock and Talk
Technique"](/doc/darknet-market/2003-steffen-druginterdiction-ch3-knockandtalk.pdf)
& [Chapter 10: "Drug Parcel
Systems"](/doc/darknet-market/2003-steffen-druginterdiction-ch10-drugparcelsystems.pdf).
(The claims in ch3 may seem improbable, but if you read through [my
census of DNM arrests](/dnm-arrest "'DNM-related arrests, 2011--2015', Gwern 2012"), particularly
Australia or New Zealand arrests, you'll see many people *do*
immediately confess.)
:::

*Daemon*, Suarez 2009
---------------------

[[*[Daemon (Daemon, #1)](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0525951113&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Daniel Suarez]{.book-review-author}[2009]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
An unfriendly AI designed by a dead billionaire (who for some reason
reminded me a little of an evil John Carmack) takes over the world. The
overall combination is good but nothing to write home about. The
frequent appearance of autonomous vehicles is a nice touch and one too
often absent from SF, near or far. Most of the technical details were
good (I was not surprised to read the short author bio and learn Suarez
is a practicing programmer, since the Ross character felt like an author
self-insert).

But the decentralized conspiracy was done better in Sterling's "Maneki
Neko", the creepy persuasive AI was done better in 'Friendship is
Optimal', the technical detail in _Cryptonomicon_, the futuristic
developments in _Otherland_, and the plutocracy/government stuff in
multiple William Gibson novels (Suarez's version being more than a
little bit crude---citing _Confessions of an Economic Hitman_,
really?).

And despite the lauded technical detail, the AI presented is farcical;
it's hard to see how any 'logic tree' could possibly handle all that
the AI does even if Suarez throws in some failures to parse responses
and writes up . (If I had an iPhone I'd probably snark about how Siri
can understand the responses given by various characters fine, and
hasn't taken over the world.)
:::

*The Midas Paradox*, Sumner 2015
--------------------------------

[[*[The Midas Paradox: Financial Markets, Government Policy Shocks, and the Great Depression](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=1598131508&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Scott Sumner]{.book-review-author}[2015]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
I picked this up to try to learn a little more about [Scott
Sumner's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_monetarism) market
monetarism paradigm of macroeconomics. I've read [his
blog](https://www.themoneyillusion.com/) for any number of years now and
Sumner's forecasting abilities and consistent application of EMH have
impressed me---he seems to predict macro trends more than most, seems
to've been right about the Federal Reserve response to the 2008
depression being inadequate and delaying recovery, right about Abe's
Abenomics falling through, right that hyperinflation was never around
the corner the past decade, right that Bitcoin could not be forecast
easily nor was a bubble (starting back ~$30 IIRC), right that the
Federal Reserve would continue to fail to cause inflation and make
ridiculous excuses. As his only book, I thought it'd explain his views
and their basis a little more to me.

It does this to some degree but is almost entirely an---at time
excruciating---detailed look at commodity and gold price time-series in
the Great Depression and the *New York Times* financial news coverage
thereof. This is a case of being so technical and down in the weeds that
I can't learn much from it or evaluate it fairly and must shrug.
:::

*Clever Hans*, Pfungst 2011
---------------------------

[[*Clever Hans (The horse of Mr. Von Osten): A contribution to experimental animal and human psychology*]{.book-review-title}[Oskar Pfungst]{.book-review-author}[2011]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
[Project Gutenberg
edition](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/33936/33936-h/33936-h.htm): the
definitive investigation into [Clever
Hans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clever_Hans), the
body-language-reading horse. Pfungst is fussy in his writing but I am
nevertheless impressed by how thorough and exacting his investigation of
Hans was, down to Pfungst mastering and demonstrating the method himself
and using little devices to trace the movement of individual body parts
on graph paper like a seismoscope (!).

There are a number of interesting
aspects which don't come up in the usual summaries: for example, a few
[historical precedents](https://www.gutenberg.org/files/33936/33936-h/33936-h.htm#Page_177) of other animals (rendering it less mysterious how Pfungst could have solved Clever Hans) and of dowsing; and that the
owner van Osten turns out to exemplify confirmation bias in a version of
the 3-2-1 game because in 'teaching', like a schoolteacher, he
methodically concentrated on one topic such as numbers until Hans
'learned' it and only then would he move on; so as Pfungst remarks, he
never bothered to test Hans on something impossible like understanding
Chinese---which Hans might have 'succeeded' on, thereby proving that he
wasn't learning anything but reading body language. Thus by a series of
constant tests, the owner convinced himself that Hans was gradually
learning each topic.
:::

*The Hye Ch'O Diary: Memoir of the Pilgrimage to the Five Regions of India*, Hyecho 1984
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------

[[*[The Hye Ch'O Diary: Memoir of the Pilgrimage to the Five Regions of India (Religions of Asia Series, No 2)](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0895810247&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Hyecho]{.book-review-author}[1984]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
<div class="abstract">
> Disappointing bland, short, and incomplete account of an extraordinary journey. Specialist historians only.
</div>

While the Buddhist Korean monk
[Hyecho's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyecho) travels (beginning 723
AD) are undoubtedly extraordinary, surely on par with Faxian or
Xuanzang, and potentially could have made for an extraordinary memoir,
his actual memoir is equally extraordinarily unrewarding for anyone but
a specialist in India or Buddhism (especially with India, where the
indigenous sources are so neglectful that a single puzzling sentence of
Hyecho is nevertheless a major find).

Quite aside from the sadly fragmentary nature of the surviving work,
Hyecho confines himself to an almanac or gazetteer level of detail,
commenting primarily on the food and diet of the locals and to what extent
the government funded Buddhism. Hyecho doesn't even mention himself
save for the occasional comment that he 'walked seven days to the next
kingdom' or, once in a while, writing a poem.

It is hard to believe a man could be so dedicated, travel so far, see so
many things, survive what must have been so many dangers, negotiate his
way through so many different principalities (how did he even feed
himself or pay for anything?), and return successfully, only to omit
almost everything of interest from his memoir. Yet, Hyecho does it.
:::

*Un Lun Dun*, Miéville 2007 {#un-lun-dun-mieville-2007}
---------------------------

[[*[Un Lun Dun](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0345495160&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[China Miéville]{.book-review-author}[2007]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
British urban fantasy; think Gaiman crossed with Pratchett's humor, but
worse than either alone. Miéville thinks he can pull off Gaiman's
bildungsroman mythic double-world shtick with Pratchett's pun humor and
put in a hefty helping of his own Marxist people power politics &
environmentalism, but he can't. The plot, after an initial subversion
of the Heroine's Journey, settles back onto track, and most of the
jokes are more wince-worthy than amusing (I did like the 'binjas' and
the 'Black Windows' of 'Webminster Abbey', and the alternative city
names like 'Not York', but that's about all). There's also a weird
tendency where the story *seems* like it's about to deliver a brutal
punch to the reader and kill off a character gruesomely to continue the
subversion of tropes, but then pulls aside at the last minute---characters tend to die offscreen and not be particularly remarked, and
the Smog delivers some very villainous monologues but fails to follow up
onscreen with appropriately horrifying actions, to be cleaned up with a
plain old deus ex machina which is unusually lazy for Miéville.

So, this isn't a particularly good book for adult fans of Miéville but
maybe it's a good book for young teens? I couldn't say.
:::

*Fear and Loathing in Las Vegas*, Thompson 1998
-----------------------------------------------

[[*[Fear and Loathing in Las Vegas](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0679785892&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Hunter S. Thompson]{.book-review-author}[1998]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
*Fear and Loathing* is famous through osmosis: the opening chapters, the
lines about bats, ether, drug collections, etc. The first quarter or so
of the novel is justly famous. The rest of it... one wonders. After
Duke and his lawyer wake up to go to the race, most of the rest is fairly
unmemorable (in particular, the two halves stitched together structure
is fairly crude.) The story is not terribly long, and it feel like the
mostly-nonfiction it is: drug fiction tends to the 'you had to be
there' kind of humor, and we were none of us there.

It also aspires to a greater import than it ever achieves, gesturing
toward the 'American dream' and finding 'fear and loathing', which
from this remote perspective, looks like bombast and bluff---we know the
Nixonian moment would pass in an epic crash, that the USSR would fall,
that the War on Drugs would not be "a boot stamping on a human face---forever". (In particular, the famous 'wave' quote seems arbitrary and
unsupported by its surrounding text, although I don't doubt that
Thompson felt those sentiments deeply.) So, the opening is fantastic,
but the rest isn't really worth reading now, for an average less than
its repute would suggest.
:::

## _Curves and Angles: Poems_, Leithauser 2006

[[[_Curves and Angles_](https://www.amazon.com/Curves-Angles-Brad-Leithauser/dp/0375711422)]{.book-review-title}[[Brad Leithauser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brad_Leithauser)]{.book-review-author}[2006]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
2006 poetry anthology; the standout of this anthology is definitely his
["A science fiction writer of the
Fifties"](https://newcriterion.com/article/a-science-fiction-writer-of-the-fifties/),
but honorable mentions to "Son",
["Nightfall"](https://www.theatlantic.com/magazine/archive/2006/08/nightfall/305030/ "'Nightfall', Brad Leithauser [2006 Fiction Issue]"),
"Four From The Forest Floor", "Equatorial Triptych", "An Icelandic New
Year's Day", ["Good
List"](https://newcriterion.com/article/a-good-list/ "'A good list', by Brad Leithauser [October 2006]").
Leithauser is a poet I can tolerate because he resists the diseases of
modern poetry, and embraces the freedom of formality; nor does he buy
into that other fatal error, the belief that poetry is not fiction but
should only be alliterative autobiography or journalism with a jingle,
and will versify on things remote from the commonplace, like
insects---who come up surprisingly often in *Curves and Angles*,
incidentally---and which he defends well in "A science fiction writer of
the Fifties": "He wrote boys' books and intuitively / Recognized that
the real / Realist isn't the one who details / Lowdown heartland
factories and farms / As if they would last, but the one who affirms, /
From the other end of the galaxy, / Ours is the age of perilous
miracles."
:::

## _An Introduction to Japanese Court Poetry_, Miner 1968

[[_An Introduction to Japanese Court Poetry_]{.book-review-title}[Earl Miner]{.book-review-author}[1968]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
A short textbook I used years ago in writing [Fujiwara no
Teika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fujiwara_no_Teika) &
[Shōtetsu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sh%C5%8Dtetsu); a whirlwind
tour of [*waka*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waka_(poetry)) court
poetry from the
[*Man’yoshu*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Man%27y%C5%8Dsh%C5%AB) to
Shōtetsu. It is not remotely as thorough as his main textbook with
Brower, *Japanese Court Poetry*, but is not intended to be: Miner
profiles the major poets and offers commentary on a few key poems,
trying to bring out the esthetics, and convey to the reader, assumed to
be a student, what there is to appreciate about traditional Japanese
court poetry---a genre so easy to bounce off & write off as painfully
plain prose sentences. He does a reasonable job in this, although I
increasingly find his translations to be a little too wordy & explain
too much.
:::

*More Poems*, Housman 1936
--------------------------

[[[*More Poems*](!W)]{.book-review-title}[[A.E. Housman](!W)]{.book-review-author}[1936]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
(4.1k words, 49 poems; fulltext available at
[Wikilivres](https://web.archive.org/web/20170721145827/https://www.wikilivres.ca/wiki/More_Poems) but unfortunately
their version is riddled with typos) A posthumous collection of A.E.
Housman's poems. The tone is more conceptual than [*A Shropshire Lad*](#a-shropshire-lad-housman-1990),
with much heavier classical and Christian influence, and not so much
emphasis on the easy and flowing rhymes in short lines (the lines are
often much longer); overall, I did not get as much out of the poems.

Verses I liked included: II ["When Israel out of Egypt came"](https://www.chiark.greenend.org.uk/~martinh/poems/complete_housman.html#MPii) (last 3
stanzas), XXII ["Ho, everyone that thirsteth"](https://www.chiark.greenend.org.uk/~martinh/poems/complete_housman.html#MPxxii), XXVI ["Good creatures, do you love your lives"](https://www.chiark.greenend.org.uk/~martinh/poems/complete_housman.html#MPxxvi), XXVII ["To stand up straight and tread the
turning mill"](https://www.chiark.greenend.org.uk/~martinh/poems/complete_housman.html#MPxxvii), XLVIII ["_Parta Quies_"](https://www.chiark.greenend.org.uk/~martinh/poems/complete_housman.html#MPxlviii); plus an honorable mention of
XXXI ["Because I liked you better"](https://www.chiark.greenend.org.uk/~martinh/poems/complete_housman.html#MPxxxi), which while not great verse
on its own, becomes moving once you know the autobiographical
background.
There are two poems in this collection that I loved: VII ["Stars, I have seen them fall"](https://www.chiark.greenend.org.uk/~martinh/poems/complete_housman.html#MPvii), and [XLV "Smooth between sea and land"](https://www.chiark.greenend.org.uk/~martinh/poems/complete_housman.html#MPxlv).
Unfortunately, they are fairly famous and much quoted, so I had already
read them---rendering reading *More Poems* redundant.
:::

*Tau Zero*, Anderson 2006
-------------------------

[[*[Tau Zero](https://www.amazon.com/Tau-Zero-Poul-Anderson/dp/1504786270)*]{.book-review-title}[Poul Anderson]{.book-review-author}[2006]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
The single central conceit is outstanding and excellent hard SF, an
interesting entry in what one might call 'time dilation horror';
there's only one central idea, however, which becomes strained with
repetition over the length of the novel (as short as it is), and while I
appreciate that Anderson tried to leaven the hard SF with real
characters and interpersonal drama, I can't say he succeeded.

For punch, some of Larry Niven's Known Universe short stories using [ramjets](!W "Bussard ramjet") may be better ("Rammer" and "The Ethics of Madness" come to mind) and I would be remiss to not mention Peter Watts's ["The Island"](https://www.rifters.com/real/shorts/PeterWatts_TheIsland.pdf) (suggested soundtrack: Cloud Cult's ["There's So Much Energy In Us"](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=udWIFQgAcYQ)), although *Tau Zero* is much more ambitious.
:::

*The Buried Giant*, Ishiguro 2015
---------------------------------

[[*[The Buried Giant](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=030727103X&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Kazuo Ishiguro]{.book-review-author}[2015]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
A not particularly successful experiment in evoking amnesia and an
anesthetized and passionless world by remixing Arthurian romance into a
darker genocide/WMD take on the conquest of England; the hilariously
cliched dialogue and prose becomes bland and the reader is anesthetized as
well.

Ishiguro does little to build up characters who have no backstories
until the end, and lacks the tension of *An Artist of the Floating
World* or [_The Remains of the Day_](#the-remains-of-the-day-ishiguro-2005) because the protagonist Axl hardly
has anything worth covering up or that he rightly regrets, stripping
away the drama of gradually uncovering the mystery.

I wound up being entertained and reading it in just a few sittings
because I kept reading in plot twists to come and in particular,
expecting Axl to turn out to be Arthur himself---because I couldn't see
how any story worth the telling would emerge without Arthur---and then
the *real* story would kickstart and the artifices be dropped. But
Arthur never does, Axl turns out to be just one of his knights, and the
real story never begins and the disappointing story simply ends.
:::

*Matter*, Banks 2008
--------------------

[[*[Matter (Culture, #8)](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=1841494186&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Iain M. Banks]{.book-review-author}[2008]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Absurdly lengthy, with one of the slowest setups ever followed by an
equally abrupt and unsatisfactory resolution which kills off pretty much
every character we might have even a faint interest in† thanks to
something which is introduced out of nowhere maybe 4⁄5s of the way
through. Banks is a great author, so of course there are plenty of
rewarding nuggets scattered through out (the haunted ex-Culture man, the
deceptive artifact, the general convincingness of 'the stage is small but the audience great' theme etc.), but much less than expected from a
groaning tome's worth of Banks. The shell world concept is not
developed or employed well (you could replace all of the maneuverings
with 2D equivalents if you wished), and the rest of the background and
concepts seem pretty stock Culture.

† eg. I was expecting the betrayal to be linked to alien machinations,
but no, it turns out to be exactly as simplistic as it seems, the ex-SC
guy's good argument to the contrary, and a complete red herring as he
dies with everyone else, the complexity of his character never ascending
beyond cackling evil.
:::

*50 in 50*, Harrison 2002
-------------------------

[[*[50 in 50: Fifty stories for fifty years!](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0312877900&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Harry Harrison]{.book-review-author}[2002]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
I downloaded it to read 'The Streets of Ashkelon' (which did not
disappoint, even though I read an earlier version in Borges and a much
later version in Simmons's _Hyperion_).

It's a fun collection of relatively light stories; in classic SF style,
each story is usually short, punchy, with a single point or idea
highlighted by the 'twist' or punchline-style ending.

This means that they are rarely subtle (eg. I see complaints online that
'Streets' is an unfair caricature and reveals Harrison's stock
atheism, but it's hard to lay out the world, story, maintain a decent
style, and also be subtle or fair in just a few pages), but that's a
price I'm willing to pay. Also on the downside for a modern reader,
Harrison shared the common SF preoccupation with the 'population bomb'
and coming Peak Oil/great dieback; neither of which seem to have
happened, thankfully, but they still irritate one to read just a little
bit.

Hence, I couldn't give this a 4 or a 5, but I don't regret reading it
since some were pretty good and I did laugh while reading some. So a 3
it is. RIP, Harrison.
:::

*Shadow Games*, Cook 1989
-------------------------

[[*[Shadow Games (The Chronicles of the Black Company, #4)](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0812533828&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Glen Cook]{.book-review-author}[1989]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Croaker and company head south, and things go surprisingly smoothly and
well in the African countries they travel through until south of the
Nile, they discover an evil empire is in their path menacing an innocent
Babylonian-style city. Naturally, _Dances with Wolves_ style, they'll
help the hapless natives protect themselves. And we get not one but
*four* of the old Taken coming back to life (Howler, Shaper,
Stormbringer, and Soulcatcher)! Oh and also a 'buried evil' a la
the Dominator. Yes, another one. That's what, the third now? Good
grief.

Like the previous book, my complaint here is that the plot is simple and
straightforward, parts of it are very ham-handed (eg. once I got over my
disgust at yes, another Taken was back, it was pretty obvious where the
crows were coming from).

The upside is that it's fun watching Croaker becoming the Captain and
growing still more.
:::

*Silicon Snake Oil*, Stoll 1996
-------------------------------

[[*[Silicon Snake Oil: Second Thoughts on the Information Highway](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0385419945&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Clifford Stoll]{.book-review-author}[1996]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
I read this not long after publication, and re-read it a year ago
weeding through my books. Between the two, I would have to give it 2-3
stars.

The good part is that at the time, he was correct in puncturing or
deflating a lot of the most hyperbolic claims about the benefits of
computers and the Internet: online shopping did have a ways to go,
kids' education was not being improved by computers (and may still not
be), etc.

The bad part is, he was correct only in the short run. On many claims or
predictions where he was absolutist, he is laughably wrong now, and we
can expect his track record to continue to worsen as time passes.
This is actually a pretty common failure mode for skeptics of
technologies: I call this the Amara effect, after Roy Amara: "We tend
to overestimate the effect of a technology in the short run and
underestimate the effect in the long run."
:::

*Memoirs Found in a Bathtub*, Lem 1986
--------------------------------------

[[*[Memoirs Found in a Bathtub](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0156585855&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Stanisław Lem]{.book-review-author}[1986]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Another Lem high-concept entry in the 'incomprehensible higher
force/universe' vein akin to *Solaris* and *His Master's Voice*, with a
military-espionage theme. It is much less successful: the military
satire is inferior to other works like *Catch-22*, with perhaps the
funniest part being the introduction's historical speculations on &
comic misinterpretations of 20^th^ century societies, and there aren't
many of the interesting intellectual confections you expect from Lem. At
his best, Lem can dispense all sorts of peculiar paradoxes or proposals
or bravura interpretations, but in *Memoirs*, hardly any appear aside
from one late in the book after a drinking party, perhaps because the
world-building is so cursory (eg. where does this giant bomb shelter get
all these resources and adult humans from in the first place?).
:::

*iWoz*, Wozniak 2006
--------------------

[[*[iWoz](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0393061434&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Steve Wozniak]{.book-review-author}[2006]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Fairly interesting, although i wish he had dilated more on his technical
achievements: he described them in enough detail, I think, to annoy the
non-geeks but just tantalize the geeks.

Steve Wozniak strikes me as a naive guy who seems to willfully let
himself be exploited because to be less exploited would entail
abandoning some idealized sense of childlike innocence (I was
particularly incensed by Wozniak handing free millions to a hedge fund
just because Wozniak had made a verbal agreement months or years before
---that the hedge fund broke!---and they came back asking for more);
Steve Jobs inadvertently comes off as a grasping penny-pinching asshole.
My opinion was modified only somewhat by reading Isaacson's _Steve
Jobs_: Jobs could be munificent and non-penny-pinching---for anything
to do with *himself*.
:::

*House of Leaves*, Danielewski 2000
-----------------------------------

[[*[House of Leaves](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=038560310X&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Mark Z. Danielewski]{.book-review-author}[2000]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
As impressive and inventive a multimedia presentation this is, it's
hard not to think that the fictional Navidson movie described by the
novel would be far more interesting or moving than the actual novel is,
dragged down by a narrator who is interesting neither in voice nor
message; 'trying too hard' is a phrase I apply to some experimental or
postmodern works, and it fits here better than most.

And are we to be
moved by this? The labyrinth is constantly described, and is the heart of the book, but one thing that goes unnoticed is that with the exception of
Daedalus's labyrinth, at the center of almost all labyrinths is---nothing
at all. When one reaches it, the only thing to do is shrug and head
back out.
:::

## _Mastering the Core Teachings of the Buddha_, Ingram 2008 {#mctb-ingram-2008}

[[[_Mastering the Core Teachings of the Buddha: An Unusually Hardcore Dharma Book_](https://www.dharmaoverground.org/dharma-wiki/-/wiki/Main/MCTB/en)]{.book-review-title}[Daniel Ingram]{.book-review-author}[2008]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Nigh-unrateable due to lack of personal experience; I was reading it because an IRC acquaintance had practiced MCTB for a while and said that the _jhana_ descriptions were fairly accurate for him, and I'm always curious about altered states of mind. The descriptions in MCTB were interesting and left me wondering what is going on psychologically in them. That said, I am not convinced the more advanced meditation is worth the effort, or even beneficial, and MCTB doesn't make much of an effort to argue that they are---"better not to start", Ingram says, and I'll take his advice on the matter. Although the concentration states sounded interesting, if I was serious about looking into altered states of minds, I would be getting back into psychedelics instead as much more repeatable and less expensive. See also [SSC's book review](https://slatestarcodex.com/2017/09/18/book-review-mastering-the-core-teachings-of-the-buddha/ "Book Review: Mastering The Core Teachings Of The Buddha").
:::

*The Judging Eye*, Bakker 2009
------------------------------

[[*[The Judging Eye (Aspect-Emperor, #1)](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=1590201698&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[R. Scott Bakker]{.book-review-author}[2009]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Bakker says he's influenced by Frank Herbert, and it's nowhere clearer
than here---the reader of _Dune Messiah_ will notice the uncanny
echos. The final sequence is the almost inevitable theft from Tolkien of
the Mines of Moria, but it's sufficiently exciting and well-done that
unlike a similar theft in _The Sword of Shannara_ I didn't put down
the book in disgust.

_The Thousandfold Thought_ was a sheer disappointment, and I did
wonder whether to continue on to _The Judging Eye_, but I don't much
regret it. The new characters are still pretty dubious (Mimara is just
an annoyance), but the psychopathic son has real promise as a very dark
inversion of Leto II.
:::

*No Country for Old Men*, McCarthy 2006
---------------------------------------

[[*[No Country for Old Men](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0375706674&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Cormac McCarthy]{.book-review-author}[2006]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Somehow a disappointment, especially compared with _Blood Meridian_.
It has some fantastic moments and writing (eg. Chigurh's first coin
toss), but wrapped in a plot which feels simplistic, cheap, linear, and
video game-like; I thought at one moment that it was no surprise that it
made what was apparently a great movie, since it read like a screenplay
version of the real novel. Harold Bloom mentioned that the
apocalypticism was a flaw, and here I would agree: it's a flaw I've
also seen surface in Gene Wolfe, and which tarnishes the stories or
novels which take up a crude---dare I say, conservative?---sort of
jeremiad against these fallen latter days.
:::

*Although of Course You End Up Becoming Yourself*, Lipsky 2010
--------------------------------------------------------------

[[*[Although of Course You End Up Becoming Yourself: A Road Trip with David Foster Wallace](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=030759243X&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[David Lipsky]{.book-review-author}[2010]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
For DFW completionists only. As DFW himself points out during the
conversations, a writer is not at his best extemporaneously; any good
work is the condensation of the peak moments of months or years. It is
interesting to see DFW express his thoughts in a rather raw, unfiltered,
and direct way, but I don't think anyone needed confirmation of the
standard interpretations of *Infinite Jest* when he already laid out all
his cards in his ["*E Unibus Pluram*: Television and U.S.
Fiction"](https://www.thefreelibrary.com/E+unibus+pluram%3A+television+and+U.S.+fiction.-a013952319)
essay on television, did they?
:::

*The Rapture of the Nerds*, Doctorow 2012
-----------------------------------------

[[*[The Rapture of the Nerds](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0765329107&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Cory Doctorow]{.book-review-author}[2012]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
A mixed bag. A third of the way through, I was ready for 2 stars, and
reading only because I remembered the preview as being pretty good; it
was reading like the worst parts of Doctorow crossed with cutting-floor
scraps from Stross's _Accelerando_ (general advice: if you haven't
read _Accelerando_ yet, I strongly strongly recommend it over
_Rapture of the Nerds_).

After suffering through the crap of the first half, I *finally* get to
the real story (recommendation: C-f to "It's the Singularity!" if you
want to save yourself the grief). This is pretty fun and decent,
although the judge scenario is far from new.
:::

*Chinese History in Economic Perspective*, Rawski 1992
------------------------------------------------------

[[*[Chinese History in Economic Perspective](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0520070682&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Thomas G. Rawski]{.book-review-author}[1992]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Fairly technical, largely not of interest to non-specialists. Some of
the papers captured my interest: the demographic analysis revealing very
high infanticide rates and mortality patterns of females was quite
interesting, and some of the papers revealed a better integrated and
more sophisticated Chinese market economy than I had expected, with less
income inequality or dysfunctionality than one gets the picture of when
reading of pre-WWII warlordism and civil war.

(I read [the online
version](https://publishing.cdlib.org/ucpressebooks/view?docId=ft6489p0n6&brand=ucpress).)
:::

*The Wallet of Kai Lung*, Bramah 2002
-------------------------------------

[[*[The Wallet of Kai Lung (Kai Lung #1)](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=5551096130&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Ernest Bramah]{.book-review-author}[2002]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
One reads this for the language on display by Bramah: the absurd
sustained Latinate circumlocutions which forever perendinate and
cunctate on expressing their simple sense. As far as that goes, it's
quite an interesting exercise and the source of a number of parodic
versions of China/Japan, I suspect. I am not sure how many people are up
to an entire sustained anthology of this, though: the stories are
relatively flimsy and one can drown in the prose while losing track
utterly of the plot and personages, which certainly is not calculated to
create charm nor cheer in the consumer.
:::

*Portfolios of the Poor*, Collins 2009
--------------------------------------

[[*[Portfolios of the Poor: How the World's Poor Live on $2 a Day](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0691141487&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Daryl Collins]{.book-review-author}[2009]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
The first 2 or 3 chapters are very interesting and enlightening on the
risk borne by the poor and how they do their best to cope; however, the
rest is generally repetitive and shows the same data in somewhat
different ways and countries, and become boring quickly---making this
one of the padded books which would've been a better read as a longform
article or essay. (One exception is the discussion of APRs and how the
loans actually are done which renders them far less usurious.)
Ironically, the appendices were more interesting than many of the
narratives in the later chapters.
:::

*A Random Walk Down Wall Street*, Malkiel 2004
----------------------------------------------

[[*[A Random Walk Down Wall Street](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0393325350&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Burton G. Malkiel]{.book-review-author}[2004]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
It's hard to believe at this point that Malkiel's views on the
desirability of indexing and not trading and the basic truth of the
efficient market hypothesis were ever controversial or not conventional
wisdom (the 1 and 2 star reviews here notwithstanding... how many
geniuses like Peter Thiel blew up betting against Treasuries in the past
few years, guys? Efficient markets FTW.), but nevertheless, he was a
pioneer. I didn't wind up learning too much from this since it's
targeted at beginners, but that is not its fault and the advice is
generally sound.
:::

*Kim*, Kipling 1981
-------------------

[[*[Kim](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0140183523&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Rudyard Kipling]{.book-review-author}[1981]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Though it's one of his most famous, I found it hard to like. It's
laden in Orientalism, picaresque almost to a fault, the Buddhism is a
little laughable for all that it might have been state-of-the-art in the
1800s (although less laughable than the ornate antique language forced
on every character), and the plot is a bit of a trainwreck with endless
pages lavished on Kim growing up only to abruptly end in a short theft
of documents and an equally abrupt and unsatisfying finish to the
religious quest that previously drove events.
:::

*Cognitive Surplus*, Shirky 2010
--------------------------------

[[*[Cognitive Surplus: Creativity and Generosity in a Connected Age](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=1594202532&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Clay Shirky]{.book-review-author}[2010]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Short, fluffy---an attempt to expand on what is a pretty short idea at
core. If you read or watched any of his previous talks like ["GIN,
TELEVISION, AND COGNITIVE
SURPLUS"](https://web.archive.org/web/20121005045511/http://www.edge.org/conversation.php?cid=gin-television-and-cognitive-surplus)
and have followed some of his other writings, there's little new here
for you. One advantage of being in book form is that he includes his
references.
:::

*Genius Revisited*, Kassan 1993
-------------------------------

[[*[Genius Revisited: High IQ Children Grown Up](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=1567500056&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Lee Kassan]{.book-review-author}[1993]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Due to length I've split my review out to ["*Genius Revisited*: On the
Value of High IQ Elementary
Schools"](/hunter "A book study of surveys of the high-IQ elementary school HCES concludes that high IQ is not predictive of accomplishment; I point out that results are consistent with regression to a mean from extremely early IQ tests and very small total sample size.")
:::

*Everything Bad is Good for You*, Johnson 2006
----------------------------------------------

[[*[Everything Bad is Good for You](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=1594481946&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Steven Johnson]{.book-review-author}[2006]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
I thought it was very short and not in depth at all; yeah, his handful
of graphs of episodes was interesting from the data visualization
viewpoint, but most of his arguments, such as they were, were
qualitative and hand-wavey. (What, there are no simplistic shows these
days?) The best I can say is: the thesis is not *obviously* impossible
or wrong a priori, but needs a heck of a lot more empirical backing.
:::

*Spice and Wolf, Vol. 01*, Hasekura 2009 {#spice-and-wolf-vol-01-hasekura-2009}
--------------------------------------

[[*[Spice and Wolf, Vol. 01](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0759531048&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Isuna Hasekura]{.book-review-author}[2009]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
I'm afraid this suffers badly in comparison with the anime: Hasekura
isn't nearly as good at conveying Lawrence and Holo's interactions as
good animators + good seiyuu, and really, that's the heart of the
story. If you can't get that perfect, then there's not much to it, and
the irritating aspects of light novels come to the fore (very short,
sketchy chapters, endings that feel almost rushed, etc.).
:::

*The Art of UNIX Programming*, Raymond 2003
-------------------------------------------

[[*[The Art of UNIX Programming](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0132465884&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Eric S. Raymond]{.book-review-author}[2003]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
(Read [online version](http://www.catb.org/esr/writings/taouu/html/).)

Not *bad* as a discussion of the ideals of [Unix](!W), but more than enough dated at this point (finished in 2004
and most of it is more relevant to before then) that I'm not sure how
much is worth reading. The CLI material is as worthwhile as ever, but
the GUI parts are totally obsolete. The case studies are also rather too
brief.
:::

*Psychiatry And The Human Condition*, Charlton 2000
---------------------------------------------------

[[*[Psychiatry And The Human Condition](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=1857753143&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Bruce G. Charlton]{.book-review-author}[2000]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Not really sure what to make of it; it has interesting ideas but so
broad that one has no idea if they're right or if Charlton is hiding
fatal evidence (he doesn't have the best reputation these days, and
this book way back when wasn't received with acclamations), and one
would have to be an expert oneself to know whether Charlton is putting
the pieces together in a licit way.
:::

*The Chicago World's Fair of 1893*, Appelbaum 1980
---------------------------------------------------

[[*[The Chicago World's Fair of 1893: A Photographic Record](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=048623990X&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Stanley Appelbaum]{.book-review-author}[1980]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
As the other reviews say: worth it for the photographs, not so much the
commentary. Some might be a little disappointed by the focus on the
neo-classical prestige buildings and not parts us moderns would find of
vastly more interest, like the first Ferris wheel, the first Japanese
building in the US, Wild Bill's show, etc., but it's still much better
than nothing.
:::

*Being Wrong*, Schulz 2010
--------------------------

[[*[Being Wrong: Adventures in the Margin of Error](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0061176044&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Kathryn Schulz]{.book-review-author}[2010]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Touched on a lot of the standard points and citations you'd see
somewhere in skeptical literature like LessWrong, but in a very much
fuzzier humanities sort of way. Couldn't really recommend it unless
you're the sort of person who has never heard of Tetlock or Quine or
the studies on eyewitness fallibility or read their Kahneman etc.---for
beginners only.
:::

*Silently and Very Fast*, Valente 2011
--------------------------------------

[[*[Silently and Very Fast](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=1936896001&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Catherynne M. Valente]{.book-review-author}[2011]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Read this on [Cosma Shalizi's
recommendation](http://bactra.org/weblog/algae-2012-09.html).

While the writer is clearly skilled, the style grates and the story
leaves me cold: the only fairy tale that really spoke to me was
Turing's (but turning his life into a fairy tale, given his death, is
almost cheating).
:::

*The Cinema of George Lucas*, Hearn 2005
----------------------------------------

[[*[The Cinema of George Lucas](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0810949687&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Marcus Hearn]{.book-review-author}[2005]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Like any authorized, lots of interesting details, gorgeous photos, and
thorough with the glaring exception of zero critical thought or
criticism or appraisal (except, perhaps, for brief discussions of how
Lucas wrote the _Star Wars_ movies drawing on friends and
acquaintances, a system which seemed to break down for the prequels---with dismal results).
:::

## _Practical Criticism_, Richards 1930

[[[_Practical Criticism_](https://archive.org/details/in.ernet.dli.2015.179256 "‘<em>Practical Criticism: A Study of Literary Judgment</em>’, Richards 1929")]{.book-review-title}[[I. A. Richards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/I._A._Richards)]{.book-review-author}[1930]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
_Practical Criticism_ is a famous bit of pedagogy in which Richards takes his college students in literature courses, and assigns them short essays explaining the poem; his point is that (a point which will be depressingly familiar to many teachers dealing with ordinary students, never mind select college students) the summaries show that the students *had no idea what they had just read*. Going through examples of that is pretty much the whole book. It is amusing demonstration of the incompetence of most readers in showing the bizarre incomprehension of the plain meaning of many poems and the extreme inconsistency of their critical evaluations thereof, and Richards makes that point at length with many examples. (Still, I remain totally unconvinced by his claim that the "tinkles" poem is of any merit!) But one can probably read the first few sets of poems & commentaries and take for granted that the rest is more of the same and skip reading the entire book.
:::

## _Shame: Confessions of the Father of the Neutron Bomb_, Cohen 2000

[[*[Shame: Confessions of the Father of the Neutron Bomb](https://www.amazon.com/gp/product/0738822302/)*]{.book-review-title}[[Sam Cohen](!W "Samuel T. Cohen")]{.book-review-author}[2000]{.book-review-date}[★★★☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
It's hard to judge this one without more historical knowledge than I have.

There's a lot of things to dislike.

Cohen has a massive chip on his shoulder about the neutron bomb and how it was misunderstood which pops up every other page (but after reading the Wikipedia article, I can't say he is wrong to be aggravated about portrayals of it---why *is* a neutron bomb so horrible? Dead is dead, and limited wars fought with 1kt neutron bombs which can't affect much past 1400 meters with minimal fallout sounds way better than dropping megatons on random megalopolises).

He had a seriously messed up childhood if half what he says is true, and one gets the disturbing feeling that he was a hard line right-wing hawk solely because he was beaten up as a kid by some other kids who were socialists, and his language about the War on Drugs is disturbingly emotionally laden and based on purity.

He seems to, like the Bourbons, 'have forgotten nothing and learned nothing'---his memoir might as well have been written back in the '60s, because he shows no awareness of why anyone would consider the War on Drugs a colossal folly, discusses freely how the first Gulf War was a disaster because Saddam could go right back to inventing missiles & WMDs (‽ my online edition is dated 2006! Isn't some sort of appendix or footnote or corrigendum warranted like "oops I completely f—ked up my beliefs, sorry!"?), talks paranoidly about Russian intentions and capabilities with almost no reference to post-Cold War developments or revelations which you think he would've been very interested in, and so on. (I don't think much of the _Wired_ editor's appendix either; how does anyone sound 'erudite' when saying a perfectly ordinary sentence on the phone? You sound like you're kissing ass with absurd flattery like that. And the '90s boom wasn't due to the Cold War ending, it was due to Moore’s law finally making computers & networks sufficiently cheap & powerful that they were worth a damn for anybody but geeks, etc.)

What is there to like? Well, he has some cute stories about the sloppy studies and research & development which went on back then, like the Minutemen silos which couldn't actually launch any when tested or about Herman Kahn's dishonest scare tactic studies or about the many compromises & sacrifices of intellectual honesty RAND staffers made. Those are fun. The part where he went to France and his explanation for the Vela incident was interesting. And I also got some rather unexpected information about an extremely obscure book [that was influential on _Dune_](/dune-genetics "'Genetics and Eugenics in Frank Herbert’s Dune-verse', Gwern 2018"), which alone repaid my time reading it. So, a wash.
:::

## _The Man Who Would Be Queen_, Bailey 2003

[[[_The Man Who Would Be Queen: The Science of Gender-Bending and Transsexualism_](https://www.amazon.com/gp/product/0309084180/)]{.book-review-title}[J. Michael Bailey]{.book-review-author}[2003]{.book-review-date}[★★★?]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Difficult to review, or even just rate.

I found some of the claims interesting, but I feel too ignorant of the topic to venture any opinion at all on the book or its claims. The fact that Bailey was trying to write a popularization or 'pop science' work which focuses on anecdotes rather than precise technical description of research means that I can't evaluate the claims on their own merits, either. The best I can say is that it's not clear who has any better theory.

Finally, after reading the  <a href="https://citeseerx.ist.psu.edu/viewdoc/download?doi=10.1.1.289.4519&rep=rep1&type=pdf" title="'The Controversy Surrounding The Man Who Would Be Queen: A Case History of the Politics of Science, Identity, and Sex in the Internet Age', Dreger 2007">62-page-long compilation of papers on the controversy</a> I was appalled by McCloskey and her allies' dishonesty & behavior, which is something I shall certainly be taking into consideration if I read anything by McCloskey in the future (and appears to be of a piece with her history, I noticed later while reading [_Strategic Computing_](/review/arpa "'ARPA and SCI: Surfing AI', Gwern 2018")).
:::

2 Stars
=======

*Solid Fool's Gold*, James 2011
--------------------------------

[[*[Solid Fool's Gold: Detours on the Way to Conventional Wisdom](https://www.amazon.com/s?ie=UTF8&field-isbn=0879464593&page=1&rh=i:stripbooks)*]{.book-review-title}[Bill James]{.book-review-author}[2011]{.book-review-date}[★★☆☆☆]{.book-review-rating}]{.book-review-meta}

::: {.book-review-review}
Downloaded for forgotten reasons, finally got around to it as some light bedtime reading.

Baseball is not one of my favorite sports, to say the least; I can enjoy basketball a little bit, and football (as long as there is an "all 20" or equivalent view, otherwise football makes no sense to me), I've done enough soccer and martial arts to enjoy watching those briefly, and probably my most memorable sports experience was a high school NY state championship match where all 100 spectators on both sides lept to their feet when after a mad dash down the rink our team scored the winning goal with a second on the clock.
But baseball?
Baseball is not just boring, it is quite possibly *the* most boring popular sport in the world.
That is probably why it is so parochial, and has so signally failed to spread globally (baseball is played only in the USA and former colonies thereof), people use baseball soundtracks [to fall asleep](https://www.newyorker.com/culture/rabbit-holes/the-rapture-of-listening-to-a-fake-baseball-game), baseball writers shamefacedly admit that it's hard to write anything interesting about it & wrap themselves in pretzels [explaining why being boring is great](https://pshares.org/blog/writing-is-like-baseball/), the MLB is forced to [experiment](https://www.bloomberg.com/news/features/2021-10-11/mlb-rule-changes-atlantic-league-tests-tweaks-meant-to-save-baseball) [with a load](https://www.nytimes.com/2019/03/08/sports/mlb-atlantic-league-rule-changes.html) [of tweaks](https://www.nytimes.com/athletic/3417955/2022/07/12/baseball-infield-shift-rule-change/) to make it less boring & [cope with competition](https://www.collegemagazine.com/baseball-is-boring/) from actually-interesting sports like basketball, and one of the hottest baseball trends is to avoid having to watch baseball & instead [gamble on it](https://www.nytimes.com/2022/04/27/magazine/sports-betting-ads.html); one statistical analysis politely phrases it as baseball games having ["the highest percentage of game segments that are played in less competitive situations"](https://content.iospress.com/articles/journal-of-sports-analytics/jsa122 "‘The competitiveness of games in professional sports leagues’, Wills 2017").
([Tom Brady](!W) was an even better baseball player than football player as a youth, but chose football because he ["always liked football once he started playing"](https://bleacherreport.com/articles/2563825-the-college-recruitment-of-tom-brady), which sounds to me like a polite way of saying baseball was boring.)
After attending 3--5 Mets, Yankees, and Long Island Ducks games (in addition to being forced to attend any number of Little League games and the occasional amateur game in Boy Scouts or school), I can safely say that I would prefer to sit on the floor in an empty room watching paint dry---as it would be quieter, the food probably better and cheap, the seat more comfortable, and hey, I could practice meditating.

But, [Bill James](!W) is the godfather of the '[sabermetrics](!W)' or '[moneyball](!W)' revolution and I am constantly hearing him invoked as an example of how hardheaded statistical analysis can undermine decade or century-old conventional wisdom, and I thought perhaps I could learn something about applied statistics from him.
And a good writer or analyst makes any subject interesting, as [Nate Silver](!W) did so well in [*The Signal and the Noise*](#the-signal-and-the-noise-silver-2012).

I was not impressed.

James's ramblings on non-statistical topics are typically not well-reasoned/supported, like his rant against tipping (outlawed? good luck with that), or are unfunny (did anyone laugh at "Olympic NASCAR Racing"?), or that advertising will collapse (you'd've made a lot of money in stocks since 2011 if you'd negated James's predictions).
James's suggestion of a rainfall unit of "inches per time", on the other hand, is a genuinely useful suggestion... so useful that it's already a meteorology unit, since replaced by "millimeters per hour" (which you measure with a [pluviometer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rain_gauge#Pluviometer_of_intensities)).

Some of them were mildly interesting, like "Justice McReynolds and Buck O'Neil".
A relatively sports-free essay, ["Shakespeare and Verlander: Why are we so good at developing athletes and so lousy at developing writers?"](https://slate.com/culture/2011/03/bill-james-solid-fool-s-gold-why-can-we-develop-athletes-and-not-writers.html) is good, but makes a point made elsewhere by a number of people like Charles Murray and in any case, I'd already read it back in 2011 on Slate.

Some of the baseball ones are too inside-baseball for me to judge.
There is surely someone for whom "The 33 Greatest Starting Rotations of All-Time In Chronological Order Apropos of Nothing in Particular", a long list of tables with brief comments, is interesting, though they are not me.
"The Expansion Bomb" sounds like it might be interesting but we are in the middle of said "bomb" and if [Baseball Hall of Fame](!W) elections have caused major controversy due to changing standards over time, the furor has not reached my ears.
The most striking part of his baseball analyses is what he *didn't* write: about [steroid abuse](!W "Doping in baseball"), of which [BALCO](!W "BALCO scandal") was but the tip of the iceberg.
(Was James unable to see what countless people saw without need of statistics, and which jumped out in the statistical anomalies that set countless headlines in the home run races, or was he just too cowardly to write about it?)

The statistics left me even less impressed.
James says he's not a statistician... and it shows.
The sabermetrics on display seems more like a child's idea of what statistics is (long tabulations of data fed into simple arithmetic formulas to generate yet more tabulations of 'variables') than what it really is (quantitative modeling of the world and decision-making under uncertainty).
His modus operandi seems to be to just pull some number or formula out of his nether regions by arbitrarily weighting some other variables after slicing them up (eg. the "Stink-O-Meter") and then give some anecdotes involving that made-up variable.
I would be thrilled to see some real statistics like linear regression, or maybe even [factor analysis](!W) for dimensionality reduction (instead of arbitrarily deciding to weight one variable 0.25 and another two variables 0.375 etc.), but realistically, James would benefit from doing *anything* which would test his made-up numbers.
He has many easy ways to do better---he doesn't have to be a hardcore statistician building elaborate Bayesian models with custom MCMC routines (although that would be nice and he could've easily started doing so in the 1990s).
For example, given his large tabular datasets, [resampling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resampling_(statistics)) (eg. for [permutation testing](!W)) is about as simple a concept as could be, is dead easy to program (even into a 1980s spreadsheet by shuffling rows/columns), and would clarify a lot about whether his formulas predict at all or correlate out of sample or are accidents or even statistically-significant given the very complicated sampling distribution of professional baseball.
And yet, he's done next to not nothing all these years.
He's certainly had enough time to up his game!

One 1983 article, "The Law of Competitive Advantage", struck me as bafflingly bad (and perhaps exemplifying why James should try to identify a little more as a statistician).
In this article, James waxes at length about phenomena such as how teams which are losing can come back, and how teams which are winning often fall back or lose, elevating this observation to 'the law of competitive advantage'.
Well, it's a law, alright---a statistical law.
Could Bill James have seriously made a career of analyzing a competitive sport and *not know what ["regression to](!W "Regression to a mean") [the mean"](/doc/statistics/bayes/regression-to-mean/index "'Regression To A Mean Fallacies', Gwern 2021") is*‽
I kept waiting for the punchline where he revealed that these cute stories about all these 'laws' and 'Plexiglas principle' and 'whirlpool principle' and whatnot are in fact all manifestations of one and the same principle, and the reader, having been fooled by some plausible storytelling, would do well to remember that any time we talk about a subset like 'winning teams' or 'top players' we are inherently talking about a *selection effect* which will create regression to a mean and need not invoke any fancy dynamics beyond ordinary randomness, and... it never came.

He does append one odd footnote:
